QUESTION NO. 1 - PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO THE CONSTITUTION
Do you approve of the adoption of an amendment to the Constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General
Court in joint sessions of the House of Representatives and the Senate on September 7, 1977, by a vote of 262-1, and on May 28,
1980, by a vote of 192-0?
SUMMARY - The proposed amendment would add a new article to the state Constitution which would prohibit discrimination
against handicapped people. It would provide that no otherwise qualified handicapped individual could, on the sole basis of that
handicap, be excluded from participation in, denied the benefits of, or subjected to discrimination in any program or activity.
VOTE:
YES - 2639
NO - 1245
BLANKS - 295
QUESTION NO. 2 and LAW PROPOSED BY INITIATIVE PETITION
Do you approve of a law summarized below, which was disapproved by the House of Representatives on May 6, 1980, by a
vote of 5-146, and on which no vote was taken by the Senate before May 7, 1980?
SUMMARY - The proposed law would limit certain taxes, and change laws relating to school budgets and compulsory binding
arbitration. It would impose a limit on state and local taxes on real estate and personal property equal to 2½% of the full and fair
cash value of the property being taxed. If a locality currently imposes a tax greater than 2½% of that cash value, the tax would
- 86 -
===PAGE 89===
have io be decreased by 15% each year until the 2½ level is reached. If a locality currently imposes a tax of less than 2½, It
would not be allowed to increase the tax rate. In either situation, a city or town could raise its limits by a 2/3 local vote at a
general election.
The proposed law would provide that the total taxes on real estate and personal property imposed by the state or by localities
could never be increased by more than 2½% of the total taxes imposed for the preceding year, unless two thirds of the voters agreed
to the increase at a general election.
It would further provide that no law or regulation which imposes additional costs on a city or town, or a law granting or in-
creasing tax exemptions, would be effective unless the state agrees to assume the added cost. A division of the State Auditor's De-
partment would determine the financial effect of laws and regulations on the various localities.
The proposal would limit the amount of money required to be appropriated for public schools to that amount voted upon by the
local appropriating authority. It would also repeal the law which provides for compulsory binding arbitration when labor negotiations
concerning police and fire personnel come to an impasse. In addition, the petition would provide that no county, district, or au-
thority could Impose any annual increase in costs on a locality of greater than 4% of the total of the year before.
The proposed law would also reduce the maximum excise tax rate on motor vehicles from $60 per thousand to $25 per thousand,
and it would allow a state income tax deduction equal to one half of the rent paid for the taxpayer's principal place of residence.
VOTE:
YES 2749
NO - 1355
BLANKS - 75
QUESTION NO. 3 LAW PROPOSED BY INITIATIVE PETITION
Do you approve of a law summarized below, which was disapproved by the House of Representatives on May 6, 1980, by a
vote of 2-147, and on which no vote was taken by the Senate before May 7, 1980?
SUMMARY - The proposed law would limit local property taxes and state taxes and would provide for increased state aid for
local educational purposes.
The act would limit local property taxes in the years 1981 through 1984 to the amount levied In the previous year increased
by the percentage increase in personal income of the residents of the Commonwealth during the previous year. The local property
tax limit could be exceeded to offset decreases in local aid, to cover shortages for prior years and to pay court judgments. Pension
and retirement allowances, payments to other governmental units, principal and interest on any indebtedness, unemployment com-
pensation, amounts required to be raised as a condition of a state or federal grant, and costs for special education programs would
be excluded from the property tax limit.
These local limits would be reduced by any excess taxes actually collected over the tax limit for the preceding year. The
limit would not apply to any municipality having a general tax rate of less than $35 per thousand of equalized valuation. The tax
limit could be exceeded by a two-thirds vote of the local appropriating body.
The cost of regional and Independent vocational schools would be subject to the same limitations.
The proposed law would also limit state taxes imposed in the years 1981 through 1984 to an amount no greater than that im-
posed the previous year, increased by the percentage increase in the personal income of Massachusetts residents in the previous
year. This state tax limit could be exceeded only to increase local aid or to assume other costs approved by a two-thirds vote of
the state legislature. The amounts necessary to pay principal and interest on state indebtedness, pensions, retirement allowances,
unemployment compensation, and court judgments, and money required to be raised as a condition of a federal grant would not be
subject to the state tax limit. The total amount of local aid for any year which would be subject to legislative appropriation could
not be less than the total amount of aid for the preceding year increased by half the increase in collected state taxes during that
preceding year. The state tax limit would be reduced by any excess taxes actually collected over the tax limit for the preceding
year.
The proposed law also would require, subject to legislative appropriation, a gradual increase in the percentage of local ed-
ucational costs paid by the Commonwealth to a level of 50% in 1984. The proposal would also require, again subject to legislative
appropriation, that school aid paid by the Commonwealth in any year between 1981 through 1984 must be at least 15% greater than
that provided in 1980.
VOTE:
YES - 1747
NO - 2187
BLANKS - 245
QUESTION NO. 4 - REFERENDUM ON AN EXISTING LAW
Do you approve of a law summarized below, which was approved by the House of Representatives on November 1, 1979, by
a vote of 83-62, and which was approved by the Senate on November 1, 1979?
SUMMARY - The law provides for increases in the salaries of members of the legislature and the constitutional officers of the
Commonwealth. The law increases salaries of members of the legislature by an annual amount varying from $1,853 to $17,923. The size of the
raise conferred on a particular Individual depends upon his position within the legislature. The law has the effect of settingthe
base salary for a legislator at $20,335, but under the law legislative salaries range as high as the approximately $55,920 paid to the
President of the Senate and the Speaker of the House of Representatives.
The salaries of the constitutional officers are increased either by $20,000, in the case of the Governor, or $10,000 in all
other cases. The law raises the annual salary of the Governor to $60,000, that of the Attorney General to $47,500, and the salaries
of the Lieutenant Governor, Secretary of the Commonwealth, Treasurer and Receiver General, and the Auditor to $40,000.
The law also amends the statutes pertaining to the organization of the offices of the Secretaries of Administration and Finance
and of Human Services and to the compensation of senior officials within those offices. It gives the Secretaries of Administration
and Finance and of Human Services greater flexibility in establishing positions and setting salaries for those under their supervision.
VOTE:
YES - 314
NO. 3669
BLANKS - 196
-.87

QUESTION NO. 5 - PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO THE CONSTITUTION
Do you approve of the adoption of an amendment to the Constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General
Court in joint sessions of the House of Representatives and the Senate on November 30, 1977, by avote of 257-8, and on Septem-
ber 18, 1980, by a vote of 179-6?
SUMMARY - The proposed amendment would limit the power of the legislature to impose certain costs on cities and towns. It
would provide that any law which Imposes additional costs upon two or more citles or towns by regulating the compensation, hours,
status, conditions, or benefits of municipal employment wouldnot be effective within amunicipality until it accepts the law by vote
or appropriation of money. Local acceptance would not be required if the legislature either passed the law by a two-thirds vote,
wealth. or provided, during the same session in which the law was enacted, that the additional costs would be assumed by the Common-
VOTE:
YES - 2732
NO - 1074
BLANKS - 373
QUESTION NO. 6. - PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO THE CONSTITUTION
Do you approve of the adoption of an amendment to the Constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General
Court in joint sessions of the House of Representatives and the Senate on September 7, 1977, by a vote of 264-0, and on Septem-
ber 19, 1980, by a vote of 162-0?
SUMMARY - The proposed amendment would change the procedure by which the Legislature declares a measure to be an
emergency law, making it effective when it is signed by the Governor. The amendment would allow the Legislature to make such
a declaration by a voice vote, rather than by a recorded vote, as now required. The amendment would maintain the existing option
allowing for a formal roll call vote.
VOTE:
YES 1091
NO - 2725
BLANKS - 363

No-School Signals - 1980-81
The decision to close schools is made by the Superintendent of Schools after consulting with
the Highway Superintendent and the Police Department to determine the condition of the roads.
The practice is to close schools only in case of extreme weather conditions or unforeseen
emergency; therefore, when schools are in session on stormy days, parents are urged to exercise
their personal judgment as to the wisdom of sending their children to school.
The following schedule of NO-SCHOOL SIGNALS will be in effect for the current school year:
TWO SETS OF TWO (2) BLASTS ON THE HORN AT THE CENTRAL FIRE STATION INDICATES
NO SCHOOL AT THE FOLLOWING TIMES FOR THE GROUPS INDICATED -
6:30 AM
Grades 6 through 12
7:15 AM
Grades Kindergarten through 5

Annual Report of Superintendent of Schools
It is a privilege to submit my
Third Annual Report as Superintendent
of Schools in Lunenburg. This report
covers the year ending December 31,
1980.
As you read the reports of the
other administrators and principals,
you will receive a more detailed
accounting of the events that have
taken place in the schools. The pur-
pose of this report is to summarize
the total aspects of the Lunenburg
Public Schools.
THE STUDENTS:
The opportunity to work in a
school system that produces such fine
students is a distinct pleasure. Our
students, on a day by day basis, are
willing and excited achievers. The
results of their efforts is evident
in the work produced, the results of
their standardized testing capabili-
ties, the beauty and thrill of their
plays, musical productions, athletic
competitions and in particular their
success as individuals in our daily
school community. Their presence is
what makes it all worthwhile and I
thank the parents of this community
for providing such a supportive home
environment for the necessity of a
WILLIAM C. ALLARD, Ed. D.
quality education.
Superintendent of Schools
THE PAST YEAR:
This past year brought us many surprises and wonderful experiences. In January and February of
this past year, we worked very hard on completing one of the goals that we had established the
previous year. That goal was to establish a five-year plan to update, renovate and improve our
buildings. On May 19th, we presented to a special town meeting a proposal for a Two Million dollar
revision of our present buildings and septic systems. The townspeople overwhelmingly voted against
the proposal but what was accomplished, I feel, is that we were able to present to the town future
concerns that would have to be dealt with. Such problems as roofing problems, failed septic
systems, poor energy efficient buildings and specific lack of facilities for handicap students just
do not go away by voting against them. They are problems that will have to be dealt with in a
different manner, but they will have to be dealt with.
I feel we ended the last year in June financially solvable and having accomplished some major
goals. We completed and submitted our Basic Skills Program Plan to the State Department of Educa-
tion. This plan was approved and through the efforts of John McCarthy, Department Head in English
at the High School, the plan was disseminated and published in the local papers. I must congratu-
late John and his very large committee for their fine efforts to produce this plan and to further
continue to be involved for its implementation.
During the past school year, we established at each level of our school system a Principals'
Advisory Committee that was made up of parents, staff and students. These committees are in their
second year of involvement with our principals and have been a source of excellent information,
production and generation of ideas and concerns. I congratulate all involved for their efforts and
94
===PAGE 97===
willingness to contribute in a positive sense. I
encourage parents and residents to become aware,
informed, and involved in this process of inter-
action with our schools.
The School Committee and the Lunenburg
Education Association negotiated a new two-year
contract. This negotiation process is extremely
time consuming and difficult. I compliment both
parties on their patience, concerns and desire to
complete this process in such a positive manner.
During the summer, through the efforts of
the School Committee to provide expenditures, we
replaced all of our sewer lines that led to our
septic systems. As a result of our investigation
for the five-year plan, we discovered that large
quantities of sub-surface water was getting into
our septic system through these sewer lines. This
is a job that would have to be done eventually
even if we replaced the entire leach field.
SEPTEMBER 1980:
School opened in September with a surprise.
We were anticipating a loss of eighty-five to one
BURTON E. GOODRICH, JR., Ed. D.
hundred students and we ended up with a loss of
Assistant Superintendent
fifteen students. During the summer and early
months of the school year, many families with new
children to our school system moved into town.
We were fortunate that our staffing decisions were correct and we started the year with all of our
positions filled. Our enrollments on October 1, 1980 were as follows:
K-5

6-8

The Town Meeting in April had approved a school budget of $4,138,975. which was an increase
of 8.78%. In light of double digit inflation, it was obvious that the School Committee had done
its job in cutting costs and reducing its staffing needs. We were very appreciative of the Town's
support for this school year.
In October of this year we had gas lines put in from Oak Avenue to each of our schools. We
are presently installing gas water heaters in each of our schools SO that we may heat our water
with gas during the times we are not operating our boilers.
During this school year we also made a greater effort to involve our citizens into our Adult
Education Program. Under the very capable leadership of Paul Romano, we have increased our parti-
cipation in this program from three hundred and forty-seven (347) in 1976-77 to seven hundred and
one (701) in 1980-81. This increase has been a very positive experience for our staff and citizens.
We appreciate the efforts of Mr. Romano and his staff.
Under the directorship of Mr. Douglas Fleming, study committees at each school and in the
community were established to seek the development of a gifted and talented program for the stu-
dents in Lunenburg. These committees are presently working very hard looking at other programs,
establishing their own needs and providing documentation and information in preparation to submit
a report to the School Committee.
NOVEMBER 4, 1980 - PROPOSITION 21:
A majority of the voters in this community and across the State passed the law whichrestricts
our tax levy to two and one-half percent of assessed valuation as the amount that can be raised by
95
===PAGE 98===
any community to run its municipal government. We are presently reviewing with the School
Committee our FY-82 Budget request which reflects a 20% decrease or an $832,901. decrease from
this year's expenditures. There is no question in my mind that this kind of drastic cutting of
our budget will force us to make many changes in the way we have operated in the past. We would
encourage your involvement and participation during this process to that we can have all of your
ideas, concerns and suggestions available to us. The implementation of Prop. 21 which was voted
by a majority of the taxpayers in this Town and in the State will require us to eliminate a number
of teaching, custodial and secretarial positions which will require us to make some very difficult
value judgments about our present programs. Part of the law (Prop. 21) has removed fiscal autonomy
from school committee budgets. Since this is happening for the first time ever, it requires all
of us to work cooperatively with the Finance Committee and Selectmen to present to the Town the
best prepared and exacting budget. All taxpayers should be totally informed on all aspects of the
budgets in the Town SO that they can vote intelligently at town meeting.
THE FUTURE:
Mr. Carey and Mr. Barney will be retiring from their respective positions as Principals of
the High School and Middle School. This Town owes a great deal to these two individuals whose
combined service is 69 years. I know that the citizens and children of this community know, love
and respect these two gentlemen. I have personally enjoyed my working and personal relationship
with both of them. They leave us with a wealth of knowledge and love for their work. I congratu-
late both of them on a job well done and wish them well in their future years. We will do every-
thing that we can to replace them with competent, dedicated and sincere administrators that
typifies our retiring iriends.
I am not in the position to establish any future goals. I feel that our main goal in the
future (at least for one to two years) will be to implement the financial constraints placed upon
us as a result of Prop. 21. We must be prepared to provide our students with the best program
that we can. To do this, we must analyze each aspect of our present operation SO as to give it
its proper evaluation as a part of the total school program. When we have done this, then we must
make the more difficult judgments of how we fund it. Hopefully when we have looked at every aspect
of our operation this way, we can put the pieces back that will reflect our commitment to a total
quality program. To do this well, we will need the efforts of all members of our community, staff,
students and municipal government.
CONCLUSION:
In concluding this report, I would pay special thanks and appreciation to all the members of
the School Committee who spend countless hours in reviewing material SO necessary to one's under-
standing of the complex nature of a school system. The hours involved in school committee meetings
to establish policy and do the business of running a school system are long and difficult. I thank
each member for this contribution and support. To my fellow administrators who have the responsi-
bility to establish a comfortable environment for our staff and students and also to carry out
policy, I thank them for their support, dedication and willingness to try and work with new and in
many instances difficult ideas. I wish to thank all the staff who perform on a daily basis, the
"true" education of our students. Their dedication and personal commitment to their work is a
direct result of the success that our students have in attaining knowledge. Special thanks go to
my very capable secretary, "Ginny" Welch, who succeeds every day to straighten out my affairs and
responsibilities and in addition keep the system accountable and functioning. Also, to Dr. Burton
Goodrich, Assistant Superintendent, who has dedicated himself to the improvement of the quality of
this school system simply because he "cares". He deserves the thanks of all of us for his efforts.
To Bob Okerman, whose dedication far exceeds his job requirements and who knows his job extremely
well. Finally, to the townspeople, who have provided my wife and I with all the respect that any-
one could acquire. We enjoy your willingness to accept us and include us in your activities.

Report of Middle School Principal
On September 3, 1980, the Middle
School opened with an enrollment of 487
students. Grade 6 had 173 students;
grade 7 had 146 students; and grade 8
had 168 students.
CURRICULUM:
In addition to the basic academic
program at the Middle School, we have
enriched our curriculum considerably in
the following two areas:
HEALTH PROGRAM:
The health program at the Middle
School, under the direction of Mr. John
Therrien, has grown tremendously and
has become a very important part of our
curriculum.
The purpose of the health program
FLOYD F. BARNEY
designed for grade 6 through 8 is to
Principal
provide students with information and
an outlet for questions to enable them
to make decisions affecting vital areas
of their lives based on knowledge and
respect for themselves.
Assistance in understanding the
decision-making process is provided
throughout the program in hopes that
the student may determine a proper value
structure for himself/herself prior to
encountering the real-life situation.
Subject areas covered are present-
ed in an organized and cumulative
manner. Material is presented in the
areas of nutrition, exercise, alcohol,
drugs, smoking, first-aid and human
sexuality. A continuous values and
decision-making program underscores all
activities.
The Middle School program provides
anexcellent introduction to correspond-
ing high school program areas where
material is presented in greater depth.
SCIENCE TRAIL:
An important part of our science
curriculum is our nature trail. The
nature trail is a path approximately
1/3 of a mile long which winds through
RICHARD J. GARVEY
Administrative Assistant to Principal
school property.
99
===PAGE 102===
The major objectives of the
Middle School nature trail are to
build in our students a love of
nature and to help them grow in their
knowledge of, appreciation of, and
respect for the natural world.
The broad theme of the trail is
the relationshipwhich exists between
the nonliving factors (sunlight,
air, water, soil) in the environment
and the living things (producers,
consumers, decomposers) in the
natural communities along the trail.
A growth in students' knowledge
of various local plants and animals
is another objective of the nature
trail.
It is hoped that the learning
experiences the trail presents will
Students on Science Trail.
help children to value their natural
environment.

Individual and group counseling is basic to all guidance and counseling functions. Through

and teachers when problems arise affecting learning. The guidance personnel are engaged in pre-
ventive measures to assist students
with the stresses of "growing up",
including struggles with peer
pressures, adult relationships, ed-
ucational demands, liking one's self,
and decision-making skills. Recog-
nition of certain behavioral
reactions from students and prompt
intervention, often prevent more
serious problems from emerging.
The counselors provide support to
students, parents, and teachers
when a pupil demonstrates classroom
difficulties or deteriorating
personal problems.
Counselors are responsible for

achievement levels by administering
standardized tests and communicat-

parents, teachers, and administra-

decline of nine (9) students from the previous year. In addition to this slight reduction our pre-

NATURE'S CLASSROOM:
During the week of October 20,1980, over
100 fifth grade students had an opportunity
to participate in a resident outdoor school
program called Nature's Classroom. (See pic-
ture at left)
Nature's Classroom, located on Thompson's
Island in Boston Harbor, is an extension of
the Passios Elementary School in that our
teachers, along with the staff at Nature's
Classroom, use the out-of-doors as a labora-
tory in which to study our natural environ-
and 104 -
===PAGE 107===
ment. Each class participates in such activities as investigating weather, live trapping, marine
life, studying salt marshes, mapping land forms and examining ocean life under a microscope.
In addition to Passios teachers, there is a permanent Nature's Classroom staff of a director,
eight environmental teachers and a camp nurse.
While there is obvious academic value of such an experience, another basic goal is to develop
a "classroom family" where sharing, mutual respect, and caring is evidenced. It has been our ob-
servation that this experience has established closer student and teacher relationships which help
the learning process throughout the year.

was to offer input from the various school
citizenry relative to the schools' operation.

FORMER HOSTAGES HONORED AT PASSIOS SCHOOL:
A special flag raising assembly was held on Tuesday
afternoon January 20,1981 .More than six hundred students
and teachers at the Passios School welcomed the return
of our former hostages with opening comments by Mr.
Ralston, followed by the flag raising. Principal's
Advisory Council members, Mark Membrino and Chrissy
Plank, assisted Donald Kenney in raising the flag to
full staff for the first time in the past 444 days. Mr.
Adams lead the entire school in the singing of a patri-
otic song to conclude the program.
MAINTAINING THE BASICS:
The Lunenburg Public Schools have developed a
standardized testing program which is administered in
selected grades on an annual basis. The grade levels
identified for complete mathematics and reading evalua-
tions in the elementary school are grades two and four.
All students in these grades are required to take eight
subtests which comprise the Stanford Achievement Test.
Last spring there were 149 second grade students
tested. The combined average grade level score for the
five subtests in reading for all second graders was 3.8.
This translates to third grade eighth month, or one full
year above the national average of students who were
tested. The reading average for fourth grade students
at the Passios School was 6.5 or just under two years
above the national average.
In the area of mathematics the combined subtest average score in grade two was 3.3 or approx-
imately one-half year over the national average, while in grade four the average was 5.7 or
approximately one year above the national average.
The Stanford Achievement Tests are not intended to be a specific determinant of student
achievement. They do, however, provide us with an annual objective indication of how our children
compare with their peers throughout the country. Over the past four years in which we have used
the Stanford Test our scores have maintained the same consistent high average.
CONCLUSION:
In conclusion, I would like to thank the many advocates for children and education whom I
have had the pleasure of working with throughout the past year. I would also invite your increased
participation in the education process as we take on the immediate challenges which lie ahead.

REPORT OF READING &LANGUAGE ARTS DIRECTOR
A number of events and programs highlighted
Lunenburg's reading/language arts curriculum and
teachers in 1980.
MIDDLE SCHOOL READING PROGRAM VALIDATED
BY DEPARTMENT OF EDUCATION:
In the spring of 1980, school systems were
invited to submit descriptions of their reading
programs to the Massachusetts Department of Edu-
cation. Validation criteria (79 items) were
established by the Massachusetts Right to Read
Effort. The purpose of validation was to recog-
nize reading programs which were exemplary, pro-
viding a model for other schools by meeting
standards in categories of: Community and School
Climate; Organizing and Managing a Reading Pro-
gram; Staffing a Reading Program; Selecting and
Utilizing Materials; Fostering Reading Interests.
We submitted our Middle School Reading Pro-
gram for validation because the validation
ROSE M. FEINBERG, Ed. D.
committee was very interested in having our
Director of Reading & Language Arts
unique program made available to other systems.
At a Validated Reading Programs Conference in October, Lunenburg and Framingham were the two
systems recognized as having met the 79 criteria supported by written evidence and by a visiting
committee which met with teachers, specialists and administrators. Lynn Fletcher (Middle School
reading specialist) and Dr. Rose Feinberg (Director of Reading) discussed the unique features of
the Turkey Hill Middle School Reading Program and distributed materials describing the program to
the two hundred educators selected to attend from school systems across the state. Mr. Floyd
Barney, Turkey Hill Middle School Principal, and Dr. William C. Allard, Superintendent, also repre-
sented Lunenburg at the conference.
Among the special features of the middle school reading program which were of particular
interest to other educators were:
- Communications Units in grade 7 (Newspaper, Career, Consumer, Research)
- Life-Skills Reading in grade 8
- Individualized approaches in grade 6
- Study Skills program developed by Mrs. Fletcher
- Guided Study approach to remedial reading (using subject-area textbooks)
- Study Guides developed with teachers in subject areas of social studies, science and
English
- Cooperation and support of principal, teachers, and reading department
The Massachusetts Department of Education duplicated copies of A Validated MIddle School
Level Reading Program: Lunenburg, Massachusetts.
PROJECT LITE IN GRADES 5 AND 6:
The English programs in grades 5 and 6 have been enriched by PROJECT LITE (Learning Inte-
grated through Esthetics), an approach integrating arts with basic skills.
- 107 -
===PAGE 110===
In grade 5, PROJECT LITE teaches nouns, pronouns, verbs, adjectives and adverbs using art,
music and movement. In grade 6, PROJECT LITE teaches creative writing, poetry and research skills
using music and art.
The classes used the program on a trial basis Winter/Spring 1980. We then applied for and
were granted a Title IV-C adoption grant. These funds enabled us to obtain the excellent materials
of PROJECT LITE for our system on a permanent basis. PROJECT LITE is used in addition to our
English textbooks and has had positive learning results.
SRA MASSACHUSETTS WRITING PROJECT:
Book, published by Science Research Associates, was introduced on a trial basis. The project in-
Grade 7 and 8 English teachers were part of a writing project (Winter/Spring) when The English
cluded free consultation for teachers and a series of professional newsletters. The editor of this
English composition series, Dr. Allan Glatthorn of the University of Pennsylvania, was interviewed
by 7th and 8th grade students when he visited Lunenburg. Students were able to meet and ask
questions of the writer of their English Composition book.
JUNIOR GREAT BOOKS PROGRAM:
In the fall of 1980, the reading department became interested in Junior Great Books, a pro-
gram of interpretive reading and discussion for students in grades 2-12. In this program, stu-
dents "develop the habit of reading critically, interpreting what they read, and supporting their
interpretations with ideas and facts from the readings.' Dr. Rose Feinberg and Lynn Fletcher
received the necessary leadership training in the fall of 1980 and will be training teachers and
volunteer parents to lead Junior Great Books groups in grades four, six and eight. Funding for
the books is supported by a Title IV-C federal grant written by Dr. Goodrich.
CONCLUSION:
Our dedicated teaching staff has responded very positively to the new programs and techniques
in the reading and language arts areas. During 1980, new programs introduced in previous years
have been implemented successfully, as well as continued improvement of basic reading/language arts
curriculum. Our classroom teachers deserve the credit for the recognition which Lunenburg has
earned in the reading/language arts area, for they are the ones who play the most important part
in the educational system.
1970 TEN YEARS AGO:
YOUR SCHOOL PRINCIPALS - Lunenburg Senior High School (Grades 8-12)

RETIREMENT
P. ROBERT CORMIER
Bob Cormier retired this year from
our school system after serving nineteen
years. Bob served as a teacher and a
department head in Foreign Languages.
of these nineteen years, the greatest
praise of his work and involvement will
come from his students. This is testi-
mony of his wealth of knowledge, pre-
paredness for his classes, commitment to
hard work and academic expectation that
brought out the best in his students.
His colleagues recognized a man of
sincere dedication and love of his work.
Nothing Bob Cormier ever did was done
poorly. He was the epitome of an indi-
vidual who knew that importance of the
role model that was necessary for his
students. His entire teaching career was
centered around this work with young people. He wanted nothing more than to prepare them in every
way possible to succeed as individuals in a complex society.
Bob Cormier's presence as a person and as an educator was an opportunity for the Lunenburg
Public Schools that was appreciated by everyone. His absence will be judged only by the great
void he leaves within our system. God bless a wonderful man!
A former Lunenburg teacher, Miss Martin died on
Wednesday, March 20, 1980 at the age of 79 at Rock-
IN
Ridge-at-Laurel Park in Northampton.
MEMORIAM
Miss Martin was born in Fitchburg on May 23, 1900,
the daughter of Ephraim and Abbie L. (Hitchcock) Martin.
She was a 1918 graduate of Lunenburg High School and
1919 post-graduate of Cambridge Latin School. She
graduated in 1923 from Radcliffe College, Cambridge,
with a bachelor's degree and taught Math and Science in
MISS MINNIE M. MARTIN
Woodstock, Vermont from 1923-1925; Stratford, Connecti-
cut from 1925-1944; and Lunenburg from 1944-1961.
She was a former member of the United Parish,
Lunenburg. Miss Martin was also a member of the Retired
State, County, and Municipal Employees Association of
Massachusetts.
She left a sisterMiss Hazel P. Martin of Northamp-
ton; a niece Mrs. Robert stafford of Lunenburg and two
nieces and a nephew in Florida.
A memorial service was held at the Moorcroft
Funeral Home in Fitchburg.

THE MARGARET PROCTOR HARLEY LOAN FUND -
The Margaret Proctor Harley Loan Fund, established by her friends at the time of her retire-
ment, will be loaned out to the second, third or fourth year college students. Further details may
be obtained from your Principal or Guidance Director.
ALBERT BALCOM FINANCIAL ASSISTANCE AWARD
.Michael J. Ciuffetti

CLASS SONG:
"You've Got A Friend"
- Carole King
When you're down and troubled and you need some love and care,
And nothin', is goin' right,
Close your eyes and think of me and soon I will be there to brighten up even your darkest night.
You just call out my name, and you know wherever I am
I'll come running' to see you again.
Winter, spring, summer and fall, all you have to do is call, and I'll be there.
You've got a friend.
If the sky above you grows dark and full of clouds,
And that ol' north wind begins to blow,
Keep your head together and call my name out loud,
Soon you'll hear me knockin' at your door.
You just call out my name, and you know wherever I am
I'll come runnin' to see you again.
Winter, spring, summer and fall, all you have to do is call, and I'll be there, yes I will.
Now, ain't it good to know that you've got a friend when people can be so cold?
They'll hurt you, yes and desert you, and take your soul if you let them.
oh, but don't you let them.
You just call out my name, and you know wherever I am
I'll come runnin' to see you again.
Winter, spring, summer and fall, all you have to do is call, and I'll be there, yes I will.
You've got a friend.

SETTLED:
1718
INCORPORATED:
August 1, 1728
TYPE OF GOVERNMENT:
Town Meeting
LOCATION:
North Central Massachusetts, bordered by Townsend on
the north, Shirley on the east, Leominster and Lancaster
on the south, and Fitchburg and Ashby on the west. It is
about 47 miles from Boston and 29 miles from Worcester.
COUNTY:
Worcester
ELEVATION AT TOWN HALL:
Approximately 570 ft. above mean sea level
LAND AREA:
26.63 Square Miles
TOTAL AREA:
29 Square Miles
TOWN ROADS:
77.51 Miles
STATE ROADS:
6 Miles
POPULATION (1980 U. S. Census):
8,375 (preliminary count)
DENSITY:
306 Persons Per Square Mile
ASSESSED VALUATION:
$77,507,240.00 (Real Estate)
$ 3,488,990.00 (Personal Property)
TAX RATE:
$54.00
